These Underrated Tools Are Up to 39% Off Ahead of Black Friday

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Shop Black Friday savings on clever, overlooked tools that make everyday projects easier–and prove that the little guys deserve some glory, too.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Every toolbox has its stars. The cordless drill, the circular saw, the impact driver. Big, powerful tools that get most of the attention. But tucked away in the corners of my own tool drawers are the unsung heroes—those little problem solvers that I barely think of until that moment I desperately need them. And when I do—Wow—I sure am glad they’re there.

I’m talking about the clever tools specifically designed for certain situations that make everyday jobs easier. A Klein Voltage Tester so I don’t mistakenly zap myself as I replace a wall receptacle. A Bosch Laser Measure that saves me from wrestling a tape across a ceiling. Or a Japanese-style pull saw that makes clean, precise cuts the old-school way—by hand. These might not be the flashiest tools in my shop, but I’ve come to dedicate a singular drawer to them as I use them so often.

With Black Friday deals already rolling in, now’s a great time to give these underrated workhorses a little love. I’ve rounded up a few of my favorite useful, affordable unsung-hero-type tools that are currently enjoying some pretty significant discounts. They’re great gifts for the DIYer who seems to have everything, and even better excuses to treat yourself to a tool that perhaps you didn’t know you needed.

Here’s a holiday tool purchase that won’t hurt your gift budget. For less than $20 on sale, this little Klein Voltage Tester deserves a spot in your handyperson’s toolbox. Touch the receiver to a live wire, outlet, or switch and the tester lights up and beeps, indicating a flow of current. I keep mine right in its own small pocket in my tool belt, and another in my tool drawer so it’s an easy grab to make sure that wire isn’t lighting me up in addition to the fixture it’s connected to. Is it a “head-turner” gift? Not really. Is it a gift that will be appreciated for years to come? You bet.

I’m a huge fan of these Craftsman Ratcheting Wrenches, and at 36 percent off, my enthusiasm is growing. There’s nothing like trying to loosen a stubborn bolt in a cramped space, where you barely have room to fit a conventional ratchet, let alone your knuckles. That’s where these wrenches shine. Each has a 90-tooth mechanism requiring only four degrees of swing to engage the next tooth, which makes work in tight quarters possible. This 20-piece set includes both metric and SAE, so you should be covered in terms of sizes across the board. At this price, it’s a lot of wrench for the money.

Ever stretched a tape measure across a wall or ceiling only to have it buckle halfway there? So much for your measurement–and your patience. At just around $50, this Bosch is a smart holiday tool for anyone measuring rooms for flooring, paint, or furniture. It measures distances up to 100 feet and comes with two AA batteries so your lucky DIY enthusiast can put it straight to work.

Nothing ruins your day quite like that little Check Engine light coming on. But before you pay your mechanic just to tell you what’s wrong, plug in this code reader. The ANCEL AD310 reads engine fault codes, gives you a bit of insight into what’s happening beneath the hood, and can clear codes as well. No batteries. No subscriptions. No Bluetooth pairing required. Just more information about what’s going on with your car or truck, which—at $30—is a pretty sweet little tool for a home mechanic, or anyone who wants to know where they stand before taking their vehicle to a shop.

If you’re building a birdhouse, repairing furniture, or taking your first shot at joinery, here’s an inexpensive way to get acquainted with a pull saw if you’ve never owned one before. This $18 saw from RUITOOL is a great entry point for beginning woodworkers–and useful as well for DIYers making small, precision cuts. Its thin blade cuts on the pull-stroke, with separate edges for more difficult cuts across the grain. At more than 20 percent off, it’s an affordable addition to anyone’s woodworking toolbox.

When I first started working at Popular Mechanics, this Klein Hybrid Screwdriver was up for grabs. I practically looked over both shoulders before claiming it. Years of working with tools have taught me to spot a good one, and this one had me at “Free”. It has onboard bit storage, nut drivers, and those bits are impact-rated to boot. Maybe it’s not exactly free, but at $20 for Black Friday, you, too, can have your own little “wait, seriously??” moment.

This is probably the most basic pocket hole jig that Kreg makes. I use mine all the time, mostly because it’s simple to work with. Maybe you’re building a cabinet and you want to hide a fastener in a pre-drilled pocket hole–or have another situation that calls for a hidden screw that won’t crack the material. Simply set the jig stop against the edge of your workpiece, clamp it in place, and use the enclosed drill bit to bore through the guide and into your wood. Perfect pocket holes every time, ready to accept–and hide–your fastener of choice.

These extra-slim pliers are great for reaching nuts and fittings in tight spots. Think plumbing repairs, awkward connections, and spaces where a conventional wrench just won’t cut it. The rubber grips are comfortable on the hands, and I like the serrated teeth in the jaws for gripping and pulling nails (there’s even a handy groove for holding nails and screws in place as you start to drive them). They’re not particularly flashy, but they are a genuinely useful problem-solver. And at nearly 40 percent off, they certainly deserve that spot in my tool drawer.

Have you ever cut a baseboard miter on an outside corner, only to discover when you go to nail it that there’s a sizable gap in the joint? Odds are your miter saw isn’t wrong–the construction of the wall corner is. Sure, there are methods for stacking and scribing lines on the trim to determine the exact angle–or you could simply reach for this Digital Angle Finder by Klein and uncover the truth in seconds. Use this once and you’ll come to realize that caulk isn’t your best friend in these situations–this angle finder is.

Pochettino Would Make Marcus Rashford His Next Son Heung-Min Son at Man Utd

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Manchester United are planning for life after Michael Carrick, and Mauricio Pochettino is the man they are looking to make their next manager.

The Red Devils have won just three of their 14 games in all competitions, including just one of their 10 Premier League games, leaving them 18th in the table heading into the November international break - their worst ever start to a Premier League season.

That form is set to see Carrick sacked, with GIVEMESPORT sources understanding that Sir Jim Ratcliffe will head a meeting in Monaco in Wednesday where his future will be confirmed one way or another. Former Spurs boss Pochettino is considered to be a leading target to replace him, with the USA boss having a $10m (£7.4m) release clause in his contract that would make him available immediately.

During his time in north London, Pochettino rebuilt the squad and saw them reach an EFL Cup and Champions League final, while they also finished second in the Premier League.

A large part of that success was the partnerships he created all over the pitch, with the most notorious being between Heung-Min Son and Harry Kane. The duo have combined for more Premier League goals than any other pairing in competition history, and most of those came under Pochettino's tutelage.

At Chelsea, Pochettino also managed to create a brilliant partnership between Cole Palmer and Nicolas Jackson to help the team qualify for the Champions League before he was rather harshly dismissed after just one campaign at Stamford Bridge.

Now the Argentine will be looking to do similar at Old Trafford, and it's clear that he already has the tools and personnel to at least replicate the South Korean superstar's skillset.

United's attacking options are strong. Bruno Fernandes is the talisman in the number ten role, while Matheus Cunha, Bryan Mbeumo, Amad, Joshua Zirkzee, and Benjamin Sesko provide experience and quality. Shea Lacey provides a wildcard out wide as a youngster, and is someone that Pochettino will love, while Mason Mount can be useful when he's fit.

But the forward who is likely to take the most joy from Pochettino's arrival is Marcus Rashford, and the manager can easily use him as the perfect Heung-Min Son clone in his team.

It was a surprise to see Rashford return to Man Utd in the summer following a successful loan spell at Barcelona, and he started the season well. He created a number of chances for teammates and looked sharp, even without registering a goal or assist.

But the England international hasn't featured since the 2-0 defeat to Atletico Madrid last month due to an ongoing hip issue, missing six games. Once he returns to fitness he will offer that same sharpness and direct running that Pochettino loves in his wingers, where he can hug the touchline, make runs from out to in, and punish teams.

He has the goal record to prove he is capable of being a gamechanger, too. With 138 goals for the club, nobody in the current squad has scored more, while the 30 goals he scored in the 2022/23 season are more than anyone else has ever managed in a single campaign at the club from the current squad.

That was a while ago, but at Barcelona he scored 14 goals and registered 14 assists last season, and he was in good form at the World Cup for England too, showing that he can deliver in the right setup. He was even described as 'frightening' by Ollie Watkins recently, showing how highly his teammates think of him.

"I can't speak highly enough of him," Watkins said on Rio Ferdinand Presents on YouTube.

"There was times (at Villa) where, like, I was on the bench, and he was keeping me out of the team, and I was frustrated, not because, not about him. It was just, I just wanted to play, but I could understand it, you know?

"There was times where, like, the PSG game, he absolutely ripped them apart. And I'm on the sideline, I'm thinking, I shouldn't come on there, really.

"Because he's, no, no, but I'm honest like that. Like, I don't have an ego. Like, I thought he was, because you read things and stuff like that, but I knew Marcus's talent, and I knew that the manager would get the best out of him. And to be fair, he did. And like, he was unbelievable. His ability is, yeah, it's frightening."

Pochettino will love what he can bring to the side, and he will love being trusted and relied upon. That could be the difference between getting him back to his best, and getting Man Utd back to where they feel they belong.

Threats to broadcasters hang over FCC nominee hearing

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump’s Republican nominee to the Federal Communications Commission faced opposition Tuesday from Senate Democrats who fear the commission could be controlled exclusively by Republicans under Chairman Brendan Carr, who they vowed to investigate in the next Congress.

Appearing before the Senate Commerce Committee, nominee Danielle Thumann Severs limited her commitments to independence from Carr, including on regulating broadcasters based on their content decisions.

Much of the hearing focused on Carr and his threats to use the FCC to punish broadcasters he disagrees with.

Sen. Ben Ray Luján, D-N.M., said that he expects Carr will not last long in his position once Democrats take control of Congress in the new year.

“Mr. Carr, if you’re listening now, know you’re going to be in this body once a month, if not more, and in the House of Representatives as well, answering questions … so we can get to the bottom of the nonsense and the cover-ups that have been taking place,” Luján said.

The nomination of Thumann Severs, who currently serves as senior counsel to Carr, has led to consternation among Democrats due to continued vacancies at the FCC. The five-member commission is intended to have three members of the president’s party, including the chair, and two in the minority.

Currently Carr serves alongside fellow Republican Olivia Trusty and Democrat Anna M. Gomez, whose term expired earlier this year. Gomez may continue to serve through next year under the law if a replacement is not nominated and confirmed.

Democrats are concerned that if Thumann Severs were to be confirmed, the commission’s three Republican members could constitute a quorum on their own, allowing Trump to fire Gomez.

Sen. John Hickenlooper, D-Colo., said Senate precedent is to move pairs of one Democratic and one Republican nominee for the FCC and that he “strongly” believed the Thumann Severs nomination should have been paired with a renomination of Gomez in order to continue having “both parties at the table.”

“A bipartisan FCC is essential to avoid things like large-scale media consolidation [and] I think also, more fundamentally, to protect a free and diverse press [that] serves everyone,” Hickenlooper said.

In addition to urging Trump to renominate Gomez, Democrats underlined the need for a second Democratic member of the commission.

Hickenlooper highlighted Thumann Severs’ close work with Carr, who Democrats and some Republicans have been critical of for comments about using the FCC’s public interest standard against television stations airing programming Carr disagrees with.

Thumann Severs’ work at the FCC currently focuses on the Wireline, Public Safety and Homeland Security, and Consumer and Governmental Affairs bureaus, rather than the Media Bureau.

Committee Chair Ted Cruz, R-Texas, has also been critical of Carr’s comments. On Tuesday, he listed the FCC’s request for ABC-owned stations to submit early license renewals alongside earlier incidents in which he said Democrats encouraged the weaponization of the FCC against conservative voices.

“The First Amendment places important limits on the government’s ability to regulate speech,” Cruz said. “The FCC possesses significant regulatory authority over communications, so there is always a risk that it could weaponize its authority against Americans with whom it disagrees.”

Thumann Severs committed to Cruz that she would base licensing decisions on “the law and the record” and not use the FCC’s authority to “pressure or punish” broadcasters for protected speech.

However, when Sen. Edward J. Markey, D-Mass., questioned Thumann Severs on whether the FCC could punish broadcasters the president disagreed with, Thumann Severs repeated one of Carr’s arguments in discussing the FCC’s role.

“Broadcasters are subject to public interest obligations as part of the public trustee model in exchange for the licenses that they hold,” she said. “As part of those public interest obligations, they are subject to many different rules and requirements, and the FCC is charged with enforcing and investigating the public interest standard against broadcasters.”

Under repeated questions from Markey about Carr’s previous remarks, Thumann Severs said she would base her decisions “in the facts and the law and the record.”

Luján also asked Thumann Severs to name issues where she had disagreed with Carr or Trump. While she said her decisions would be her own, she declined to name any examples of disagreement.

In addition to questions about Carr, Democrats asked the nominee about the FCC’s E-Rate program, which provides low-cost connectivity for schools and libraries, and the Universal Service Fund, which funds the E-Rate program and other programs.

Thumann Severs said that she believes the E-Rate program has been successful and that the FCC has a statutory obligation to provide some kind of discounted service to schools and libraries. But she was less forthcoming on whether the FCC could shut down the program as it currently exists without congressional approval or whether the program should be restricted only to rural areas.

Late last month, Democratic members of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, led by Communications and Technology Subcommittee ranking member Doris Matsui, D-Calif., sent a letter to Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., and Cruz urging Gomez’s renomination with any move to confirm a Republican nominee.

“Commissioner Gomez has shown the experience and judgment to continue that work,” the members wrote. “We therefore ask that the Senate pair any new Republican nominee with her renomination to a full term, consistent with the bipartisan design Congress built into this agency.”

On Monday, the Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights also sent a letter to committee leadership urging a delay on the hearing and any vote on Thumann Severs’ nomination until Gomez can be renominated.

“Independent agencies should not be extensions of presidential will: their leaders should exercise independent judgement based on their expertise and statutory mandates,” the group wrote.

Cruz did not indicate Tuesday when the committee might vote on Thumann Severs’ nomination, and no markup has yet been announced by the committee of hers’ and those of three others for two posts at NASA and one at the Transportation Department who also testified.

Cam Skattebo on Giants not adding a running back: They know what I can do

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Cam Skattebo is set to return to the Giants lineup for Thursday night’s game against the Commanders and he won’t be looking over his shoulder at any new additions to the team’s running back depth chart.

The Giants were reportedly bidders for Jonathan Taylor at one point and they were linked to other potential moves in the backfield, but they stood pat on deadline day. Skattebo said on Tuesday that he understands why some would be “skeptical” that the Giants have enough at running back while citing his average of around 60 rushing yards per game, but that he wasn’t bothered by any interest the Giants had in other backs because “they know what I can do”

“You’ve seen what I can do,” Skattebo said, via Darryl Slater of NJ.com. “When I come out and I’m ready to play football, I can be one of the most dangerous backs in the league. Simple as that.”

Skattebo was up to full practice participation Tuesday after missing Week 9 with a hamstring injury, so he should have every opportunity to prove himself right about what he’s capable of doing for the Giants offense.

Fujino’s double sparks WCL comeback win for Manchester City against Inter

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Manchester City’s European campaign finally came roaring into life as they put four goals past Inter to record their first Women’s Champions League victory of the season and greatly improve their prospects of reaching the knockout stages.

The English side had to come from behind after a slow start but two goals from Aoba Fujino put them ahead before half-time, and second-half efforts from Kerstin Casparij and Grace Clinton gave them a 4-1 victory in Monza that doubled their points tally, after their draws against Bayern Munich, Real Madrid and Paris Saint-Germain.

The last two of those draws were frustrating results for Manchester City on home turf but they were far more fluid and clinical on the road, in the second half particularly, despite deploying a much-rotated team.

With more than half an eye on Saturday’s pivotal meeting with their domestic title rivals Chelsea, Andrée Jeglertz rested several of his key players, including their top-scorer Khadija ‘Bunny’ Shaw, the in-form winger Lauren Hemp and the ever-dependable midfield metronome Yui Hasegawa, all of whom were substitutes. They were joined on the bench by Rebecca Knaak, and therefore – with England’s Alex Greenwood missing the trip altogether with an ankle injury – Gracie Prior was handed her first Champions League appearance this season, starting at left-sided centre-back.

Prior was beaten in the air by the sheer speed and precision of Ivana Andrés’ pinpoint cross when Inter opened the scoring through Elisa Bartoli’s header, as the hosts took the lead somewhat against the run of play. Falling behind against an Inter team who have not conceded in any of their six Women’s Serie A matches this season was not an ideal situation for the English side to find themselves in, but they remained patient.

Chelsea were forced to settle for a point after being held to a goalless draw against Hacken in their Women’s Champions League clash.

The Blues had two goals disallowed in a frustrating first half in Gothenburg, while Jennifer Falk made some important stops throughout the contest. Sonia Bompastor's side remain unbeaten in the Champions League, but ultimately slipped to their second draw from four outings.

Wieke Kaptein headed home for Chelsea in the opening minutes, but the goal was chalked off when Lexi Potter was ruled to have fouled goalkeeper Falk.

Chelsea dominated possession and soon had the ball in the net again when Melvine Malard hit a low strike into the bottom corner, but the forward was marginally offside in the buildup.

Maika Hamano’s looping strike bounced off the crossbar during a late surge from Chelsea but Hacken defended their box well deep into stoppage time to clinch a valuable point.

In the other early game, Real Madrid beat Paris FC 3-0 with all the goals arriving in the second half including from substitutes Lineth Beerensteyn and Linda Caicedo. Madrid rose to sixth above Paris Saint-Germain and Bayern Munich, who faced each other later on Tuesday at Parc des Princes.

Roma defeated Servette 2-1 larer on Wednesday and Bayern Munich rose to second in the standings behind Barcelona by winning 2-1 at Paris Saint-Germain. The top three in the 18-team table at the start of play were Barcelona, OL Lyonnes and Arsenal who all play later in the week. Barcelona hosts Arsenal on Thursday. Agencies

The visitors took a while to click in attack but equalised nine minutes after going behind, when Fujino timed her run perfectly to latch on to Laura Blindkilde Brown’s classy, defence-splitting through ball. Fujino slotted in her second with ease from close range, just a minute before the end of the first half, on the rebound after a Mary Fowler effort was only half-cleared.

Despite having taken the lead, Jeglertz called upon two members of his cavalry – Hemp and Knaak – at half-time, continuing to manage other regular starters’ match minutes: Fujino was given a rest, along with Jade Rose.

Inter almost levelled three minutes after the restart but Ayaka Yamashita did superbly well to get a fingertip to Haley Bugeja’s bending effort, which looked destined for the bottom corner. The WSL champions were looking the stronger, though, aided by fine performances from Blindkilde Brown and Sam Coffey in midfield.

No topic is too small or too big for us to cover as we deliver a twice-weekly roundup of the wonderful world of women’s football

Coffey delivered a clever ball into the box to find Casparij through the middle and Casparij’s finish into the corner gave the English side a two-goal cushion. They began to turn on the style from then, spreading the ball into wide spaces much quicker than they had done earlier in the contest, embracing the lively movement of Hemp and Iman Beney.

Hemp, who had three goal contributions in her previous two appearances, dinked a delightful cross to the far post for Clinton to make it 4-1 from a tight angle, with Hemp’s delivery leaving the travelling fans purring. Her direct runs at the Inter right-back were a major worry for the hosts.

Yamashita pulled off her second world-class save of the night, this time a quick reaction save, to deny Michela Cambiaghi from close range, as Inter attempted to claw their way back into the match in the closing stages but City’s game-management was more than adequate as they calmly completed an important away victory. They will head to Stamford Bridge in a rich vein of form for one of the most important-looking WSL fixtures of the year.

Meta warns some of its AI glasses may get 'too warm' if they get wet

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The company is offering free replacements to affected devices.

Meta is rolling out a software update for its AI glasses to fix an issue that causes some devices to become "too warm" when they get wet. The company describes it as a "rare issue" affecting only a "small number" of its glasses, but is nonetheless urging everyone with a pair of Meta Glasses to update their software and offering free replacements to those affected.

The company has begun notifying users about the forthcoming update which will roll out to everyone with a pair of Meta-branded frames, which includes Adventurer, Fury, Starfire, Nova and Capri models. Oakley and Ray-Ban glasses are not impacted, according to Meta.

While Meta doesn't use the word "overheating," the company compares its glasses fix to how smartphones automatically shut down when they get too hot. "We've implemented a software update to fix a rare issue that caused a small number of Meta Glasses to get too warm when moisture entered the device," a Meta spokesperson said in a statement. "It works similarly to a phone shutting off automatically when it needs to cool down. If people experience this, they can contact Meta Customer Service for a replacement."

In an email to users reported by 9to5Google, Meta says that the heat-related issue can be triggered by "excessive moisture or sweat" entering the glasses. That suggests that at least some of the company's own AI glasses may not be as water resistant as they're supposed to be. According to Meta, all of its AI glasses come with IPX4 water resistance which "means your glasses are safe if you get caught in light rain while you wear them."

The company hasn't indicated how many pairs of glasses might be affected, but says that those who experience the moisture-related shutdowns will be able to get a free replacement pair of glasses. Affected glasses will show a push notification if they do turn off unexpectedly, and the alert will direct them to Meta's customer support.

In the meantime, the company is urging everyone with a pair of Meta Glasses to update to the latest software, version 130, to ensure "the best performance." You can update your glasses from the Meta AI app by heading to Settings > Glasses > Updates. The version 130 update is still rolling out so it may take a day or so to hit everyone.

Have you experienced a pair of Meta Glasses getting too hot or unexpectedly shutting down? Contact the author at karissa [at] engadget.com or karissabe.50 on Signal.

Financial institutions confront the complexities of scaling AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

AI in financial services is moving beyond the pilot phase as banks, capital markets firms, mortgage lenders and other organizations begin deploying the technology more broadly. The challenge is shifting toward managing AI responsibly at scale while meeting strict requirements for security, compliance and customer trust.

The AWS Financial Services Symposium highlighted that transition in May. As financial institutions expand their use of AI, they have opportunities to test new applications without committing to large-scale deployments, according to Scott Mullins, former general manager of AWS Worldwide Financial Services at Amazon Web Services Inc.

“You can spin this stuff up really quickly; you can experiment with it,” he said during the symposium. “You can show that it actually achieves the things that you want to achieve within specific policy guidelines and frameworks, and you can actually evaluate whether that governance is holding.”

This feature is part of SiliconANGLE Media’s ongoing coverage of the AWS Financial Services Symposium. (* Disclosure below.)

Moving AI from experimentation into production requires financial institutions to address a different set of challenges. Beyond demonstrating that the technology works, organizations must establish the governance and operational safeguards needed to support AI across regulated business processes, according to Paul Nashawaty, practice lead and principal analyst for application development at theCUBE Research.

“Our AppDev research shows that 92% of organizations now integrate AI into at least one stage of the software delivery lifecycle, yet only about one-third have established the governance, security, and operational maturity required for production-scale AI,” he said. “In banking, capital markets and insurance, the gap is even more pronounced because compliance, explainability, and customer trust are non-negotiable. The next phase of AI adoption will be defined less by model performance and more by platforms that embed policy controls, auditability and security into AI-enabled workflows while improving customer experiences and operational efficiency.”

Infosys Ltd. is addressing those requirements through its AI platform, which incorporates safeguards and controls intended to help financial institutions deploy AI while managing risk, according to Rajaram R.K., vice president and head of sales for financial services at Infosys.

“It has the securities [and] it has the guardrails. It has the token economics … all the capabilities that an organization would need,” he told theCUBE.

Financial institutions face stringent regulatory requirements, making security, auditability and recovery essential to their operations. As banks, insurers and capital markets firms expand their use of AI, those requirements take on additional importance, particularly when automated systems begin influencing business decisions. Despite these challenges, financial institutions are among the sectors leading the way in AI adoption.

“The organizations that treat AI as a regulated business capability, not simply a technology initiative, will be best positioned to accelerate innovation while maintaining resilience and meeting evolving regulatory expectations,” Nashawaty said.

Applying those established risk-management practices to AI requires organizations to consider how automated systems access data, make decisions and operate within existing policies. The challenge is extending governance and operational controls to AI-enabled workflows without undermining the efficiency and innovation those systems are intended to deliver.

Financial institutions are finding practical applications for AI in customer service and employee productivity. At New York Life Insurance Co., reducing routine manual work is giving employees more time to provide personalized service and respond proactively to customers’ needs, according to Kristina Welke, vice president at New York Life.

“We really felt there was a lot of opportunity to support them, remove the manual work they do every day, enable them to provide that consultative support, be proactive versus reactive,” she told theCUBE.

Fifth Third Bank is using conversational AI to improve customer interactions and reduce average call-handling time. The bank is using Amazon Connect to support its customer-service operations, according to Kevin Anderson, product manager on the AI transformation team at Fifth Third Bank.

“Amazon Connect really lays the foundation as it solves for not only messaging but voice as well, allows for AI and automation,” he explained.

For financial institutions, scaling AI depends in part on modernizing the infrastructure that supports data-intensive workloads. Cloud platforms can provide the flexibility to deploy new capabilities while maintaining access to existing systems, making modernization an important consideration as organizations expand their AI initiatives.

Mortgage lender PennyMac Financial Services recently expanded its agreement with Amazon Web Services to accelerate modernization of its proprietary mortgage servicing platform, Plaisse. The partnership also includes plans to deploy enterprise-grade AI capabilities and further develop the platform.

As financial institutions modernize their infrastructure, they also need ways to move and manage data without disrupting existing operations. NetApp Inc. is addressing those requirements through storage and data-management capabilities designed to support migration, resilience and performance in AWS environments, according to Jacob Lim, cloud solutions architect at NetApp.

“We create value for customers by making it simple to migrate and run their data in AWS by combining proven NetApp ONTAP capabilities, such as space-efficient snapshots, near-instant data cloning capabilities with minimal storage overhead and storage performance,” he told theCUBE. “Then, there is also the highly efficient data replication and ransomware detection engine natively built into the operating system directly. Combined with AWS scale and elasticity, customers gained resilience, performance and operational simplicity in the cloud.”

Scaling AI in financial services brings new challenges

As financial institutions incorporate AI into daily operations, managing the complexity of those deployments is becoming an increasingly important challenge. According to PagerDuty’s 2026 State of AI-First Operations report, 59% of surveyed banks were actively incorporating AI into their operations, while another 34% were discussing implementation. As adoption expands, institutions must determine how to govern these systems, maintain data quality and integrate AI into existing workflows.

“I think you’re going to see AI really become: How do we govern, how do we use [and] how do we make sure the data’s clean? How does it become [about] helping us be more efficient in what we’re doing in day-in and day-out operations?” Todd McNabb, chief revenue officer of PagerDuty Inc., told theCUBE.

Technology providers are responding to the demand for faster enterprise AI deployment. AWS’ recently announced $1 billion investment in forward-deployed engineering, for example, is intended to help customers develop and deploy agentic AI applications more quickly. For financial institutions, however, the measure of success will extend beyond deployment speed to how effectively AI can operate within existing systems, regulatory requirements and business processes.

The 8 Best Beginner Snowboards of 2027

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

What to know: This guide covers the eight best snowboards for beginners, including the Editors’ Choice Salomon Lotus (women’s) and Salomon Pulse (men’s). Testers evaluated the boards based on how well they flex, carve, and hold an edge. Keep reading for our tips on choosing the best snowboard if you’re just starting out or are looking to progress.

Bunny hills might not be steep, but the snowboard learning curve definitely is. You’ve got to pay your dues and eat shit snow a couple times. But after a lesson or two and some good old-fashioned repetition, things will start to click.

One of the biggest mistakes beginners can make is riding the wrong equipment. We constantly see beginners riding boards that are too stiff, too big, or too small. That’s why we pulled together this guide: to help you find the right snowboard for you, so that the path from beginner to intermediate is a little smoother—and a little less painful.

This past spring, we put 44 snowboards to the test. We asked our crew of snowboard testers—including instructors and coaches—to identify boards that were easy to ride and approachable for beginners. Below, you’ll find the result of months of testing and countless laps in the Sierra, Wasatch, and Rockies: our favorite beginner snowboards of 2027.

Sizes: 142-163 cm (Pulse); 135-155 cm (Lotus) Genre: All-mountain

Pros and Cons + Versatile and fun + Beginner-friendly shape + Encourages learning fundamentals – Not ideal in choppy conditions – Overwhelmed by high speeds

If you’re looking for a snowboard that will help you learn solid fundamentals without encouraging bad habits, the Salomon Lotus (women’s) and Pulse (men’s) are hard to beat—and easy to ride. They’re soft-flexing and approachable, proving to be “exceptionally intuitive for any skill level,” according to a Tahoe tester.

Gentle camber underfoot supplies grip in firmer snow, and a touch of pop makes it approachable to learn how to ollie and cruise through the park. The Pulse and Lotus are also directional twins, with a bumped back stance for float in powder and stability at speed, while the nose and tail are the same width for solid switch handling.

In slush and hardpack, one tester appreciated the camber underfoot, which flows into a rockered nose and tail. “You would have to try very hard to catch an edge. This is a confidence-building board,” she reported. The Pulse is similarly predictable, stable, and easy to manipulate.

The Salomon decks showed their weaknesses at high speeds, on bumpy, icy snow, and in technical moguls—terrain and conditions most beginners will want to avoid anyway. For newer riders, though, this board is an ideal first buy.

Sizes: 146-161W cm Genre: All-mountain freestyle

Pros and Cons + Easy to turn + Buttery, playful for stepping into the terrain park + Rides switch well – Soft flex and rocker-dominant profile make it less of a long-term investment

For a solid beginner board that doesn’t break the bank, the Rossignol Ampage Vol. 2 is an easy-riding, rocker-dominant, twin shape that rides regular and switch equally well.

The Ampage is soft—way too soft for riding hard off-piste—but the flex is perfect for learning the ropes, practicing on groomers, and getting a feel for buttering, jibbing, and jumping in small terrain parks. “Both the nose and tail are flexible for buttering,” a Tahoe tester reported, speaking to the Ampage’s inherent playful nature. The rocker is designed to make turns feel smooth and catch-free, while a touch of camber adds moderate grip while carving.

Even at full retail ($400), the Ampage is a decent deal. But last year’s version is currently on sale, making it an incredible steal for boarders on a budget. Because of the core malleability and easy-riding rocker profile, progressing beginners might outgrow this board more quickly than other decks on this list.

Sizes: 150-167W cm Genre: All-mountain/Freeride

Pros and Cons + Floaty freeride shape + Playful freestyle chops – Not as reliable straightlining – Undergunned in chop

The redesigned Jones Frontier 2.0 is softer, more buoyant, and friendlier than ever, making it a solid option for powder-chasing beginners, intermediate riders learning to navigate the deep stuff, or cruisy shredders who appreciate surfy, laidback turns.

The exaggerated, elongated, rockered nose and a touch of taper (8 millimeters) help the Frontier perform well in powder, while three-dimensional contouring facilitates smooth and natural carving. Camber beneath your boots, a medium sidecut, and Jones’ serrated Traction Tech edges help the shape bite into piste on rail. “It lays sweet, soft trenches, and allows for nuanced wiggling underfoot,” applauded a hard-to-please Colorado tester who was surprised by how much he enjoyed the Frontier’s cruise control.

An approachable combo of biaxial fiberglass and a poplar and paulownia core yields a softer, predictable flex pattern. Testers had mixed feedback on the resulting ride. Some found the Frontier 2.0 “effortless and forgiving.” Multiple harder-charging riders called it a “dad cruiser,” with a 30-year-old ripper joking he’d happily buy it—in 20 years. But for beginners and intermediates especially, that flex makes for an intuitive, easy-to-direct deck that facilitates flowy turns and forgiving forays into powder.

Sizes: 151-170W cm (men’s); 139-154 cm (women’s) Genre: All-mountain freestyle

Pros and Cons + Approachable flex + Decent float in powder yet still solid riding switch + More butter than Land O’Lakes 𑂾 Overwhelmed at high speeds in variable conditions 𑂾 Not the most reliable for bigger drops

The Jones Rally Cat combines a capable, all-mountain directional twin shape (a touch of setback, zero taper, full camber, and spooned-out nose and tail) with a softer, forgiving flex pattern. The pliable feel comes from a poplar/paulownia wood core and a dual-direction, dual-layer, stitched fiberglass laminate.

A progressing, all-mountain Tahoe tester judged the flex “loose enough to be playful but stiff enough to hold an edge.” The shape inspired confidence and elevated her riding to a “more fun level,” and she recommended the board for “new riders, park riders, and playful freeriders.”

Despite its setback stance, the Rally Cat’s lack of taper made it equally adept at jibbing through the park and riding switch, according to one Mammoth snowboard coach. She also dug the tight, 6.6-meter sidecut for the 148-centimeter length we tested. Combined with the spoon-shaped nose and tail, she said the Rally Cat facilitates “effortless turn initiation and quick and efficient edge-to-edge transitions.”

However, a harder-charging North Lake Tahoe freerider and snowboard instructor warned that the buttery flex in the tail that makes the Rally Cat a blast on side hits and park laps can be overwhelmed by overhead drops and chop. This won’t be an issue for true beginners, but worth keeping in mind as you progress.

Sizes: 151-164X cm (men’s); 139-151 cm (women’s) Genre: All-mountain

Pros and Cons + Versatile twin + Most upside for progression out of any board in this guide + Durable build – Expensive – Too much for true beginners

Never Summer’s Proto Type 3 is more expensive and stiffer than the other options in this guide, but it’s also more stable and reliable, providing excellent edgehold, carving chops, and a solid backbone for handling sizeable jumps, cliff drops, and higher speeds. It’s too much for true beginners, but it can be a smart investment for late-stage beginners committed to progression and who want a board they can use for years to come.

The all-mountain twin features a classic Never Summer camber concoction, utilizing three camber bumps running from inserts to inserts, with rocker at the nose and tail. Combined with an adaptable sidecut and a medium-flexing, carbon-bolstered poplar-paulownia-birch core, the Proto Type 3 rips on edge. “Edgehold feels very locked in,” said a Tahoe tester. “Incredibly balanced and easy to shift from toe to heel,” commented a Mammoth rider who appreciated the Proto’s ability to tackle smaller to larger turns well.

The carbon reinforcements help the Proto Type 3 pop aggressively off lips like pubescent pimples. “Stable through the air even on the Palisades Gold Coast jump line,” commented a Tahoe freestyler who also praised the camber for keeping him upright on tail-heavy landings and the rubber laminates and UHMW sidewalls for dampening. The Mammoth rider agreed, saying the board smoothed out bumps that typically irritate her bad knees. Her verdict:  “After you’ve mastered making it down a green run, this board would be great for a late beginner to intermediate rider looking to perfect carving and increase confidence.”

Pros and Cons + Wide ride is perfect for riders with bigger boots + Turns confidently and floats well + Solid freestyle flavor – Can be sluggish compared to narrower boards

The Cardiff Lynx is a wide ride that’s a game-changer for boarders with bigger boots who have a penchant for powder. However, it’s more suited to progressing, late-stage beginners and aspiring intermediates than day-one newbies.

The generous width combined with rocker in the nose and tail, as well as a touch of taper, help the Lynx float extremely well in powder. A versatile, blended sidecut enables the Lynx to tackle a variety of turn sizes for true all-mountain capability, while subtle serrations along the edges help grip firm snow like the claws of the Cardiff’s namesake. “Edges lock into turns,” voiced a Tahoe snowboard instructor. “High-speed carving is fun.”

The Lynx is Cardiff’s softest offering, with a pliable cedar and aspen core that can handle rails, boxes, butters, and playful small-to-medium jumps. One Tahoe tester loved taking the board all over the mountain with a freestyle approach, noting that it handled switch riding better than expected for a tapered shape with a setback stance.

That said, the flex is stiffer and ultimately more capable in steeper all-mountain and freeride terrain than many of the other decks in this guide, although the Lynx’s build is not the most shock-absorbing in chunky moguls. Not to mention, that width can make the Lynx sluggish at slower speeds. But no one took issue with the cool graphics— testers loved the black, big cat-emblazoned topsheet.

Fit Tip: To make sure the Lynx will work for your boots, check the technical specs. In particular, look at the underfoot width at the front and rear foot. Compare that to the length of your boot sole, remembering that your boots may be turned slightly at an angle, depending on your stance preferences.

Sizes: 148-161W cm Genre: All-mountain freestyle

Pros and Cons + Approachable all-mountain cruiser + Excels hitting rails and boxes + Fun, buttery, and easy to maneuver – A bit soft for high-speed all-mountain ripping and freeriding

If you’ve already fallen in love with sliding boxes and rails, CAPiTA’s Outerspace Living might be a perfect fit. This directional twin rides switch well, with a sliver of setback for all-mountain riding en route to the park, so it can easily be your daily driver. It features gentle camber underfoot for pop and hardpack proficiency. Rocker in the nose and tail reduce common catch points while carving and facilitate easy jibbing and buttering. “It’s forgiving and light,” commented a Tahoe park regular. “You won’t catch an edge easily.”

The board’s minimal weight comes courtesy of a slender, malleable poplar and beech core. Despite carbon strips running parallel down the length of the deck, it rides on the softer side. Our testers loved that flex while hitting rails, spinning lazily off rollers, and sniffing out side hits, but our most advanced riders overpowered the Outerspace Living at high speeds in steeper terrain. It’s a great tool for playful all-mountain riders looking to develop their jib skills, or progressing riders looking for a stepping stone to more advanced boards.

Sizes: 135-162W cm Genre: All-mountain, park, powder

Pros and Cons + Great pop and landing gear for jumps + Decent jibber + Rides switch well – Too much for true beginners – Not our favorite in powder

When one of our crew test-drives a deck and instantly buys one for themselves, it’s a damn good sign. And that’s precisely what happened when one of our Mammoth testers put the Burton Good Company to use in her local terrain park. A true twin with camber underfoot and a slightly stiffer-than-advertised medium flex, this is a board that loves to snap ollies, power off lips, and stomp landings with authority.

“Handled medium-sized jumps like a dream,” reported that Mammoth rider, noting she felt confident and comfortable in the jump line. “Riding switch was a breeze,” voiced another Mammoth tester, who appreciated the Good Company’s grip on edge, which translated to boosting prowess in the mini pipe, too. She was also pleased by the board’s ability to spin on and off rails.

Because the Good Company felt a little stiffer than advertised, testers said it’s better for more advanced beginners or intermediates interested in park jumps. It’s also more capable than anticipated in all-mountain terrain, preferring medium and large radius turns to quicker, tighter ones. It doesn’t have a penchant for buttering, and it’s not our top pick for powder. But for progressing all-mountain riders, the Good Company delivers at a very fair price.

Shopping for a snowboard can be overwhelming, especially as a beginner. We’re not going to dive too deep on snowboard tech here (you can check out our additional snowboard coverage here for that), but we want to quickly help you understand a few key concepts discussed in this guide.

“Beginner” is a broad and admittedly unhelpful phrase. If you’re a true first-timer or close to it, you should focus on the first four snowboards in this guide. The best beginner snowboards are on the softer side, making them more approachable and easier to learn the basics on. They’re also generally a little cheaper than the next four boards we’ve included in this guide, which are boards we recommend for more experienced beginners, those approaching intermediate status, or riders who are hellbent on progression or excited to explore a certain style of riding.

Snowboards come in all shapes and sizes, but they can be considered true twins, directional boards, or directional twins.

True Twins, like the Rossignol Ampage, have a symmetrical nose and tail, which look the same and have the same flex. This ensures that the board feels the same whether you’re riding regular or switch. Twins are popular with park riders, as riding switch is a keystone of freestyle snowboarding. True twins are also a sweet choice for beginners, as they make learning “falling leaf” down steeper slopes a piece of cake.

Directional snowboards, like the Jones Frontier 2.0, have a purpose-built nose and tail. They’re designed to be ridden primarily in one direction, with the nose pointing downhill. They often have bigger, floatier noses and shorter, stubbier tails, a combination that can supply stability at speed, buoyancy in deep snow, and landing prowess. Directional snowboards often have taper, which means the nose is wider than the tail, and a setback stance that is bumped toward the back of the board. Both setback and taper can contribute to float and stability.

The main downside of directional boards is that they don’t ride switch nearly as well, which explains why many freeriders and all-mountain riders prefer directional boards, but freestylers stick to twins.

Directional twins combine certain elements from twin boards and others from directional boards to create hybrids that perform across a variety of conditions. For instance, the Jones Rally Cat is a directional twin in the sense that it features a nose and tail that are exactly the same width, enabling it to ride switch well, like a twin. But the nose is longer than the tail, helping the Rally Cat to float in powder and tackle steeper terrain like a directional board.

When you lay a snowboard flat on the ground and look at it from the side, you’re getting a feel for its profile. Profile dictates much of a snowboard’s riding qualities, as it influences how you weight, flex, and engage the board’s sidecut on snow.

Cambered snowboards, like the Burton Good Company Camber, look like an upside-down “U” when you lay them flat on the ground. There’s a raised central section between your feet, and two points of contact with the ground toward the nose and tail. Camber provides grip when you’re carving on firm snow, reliability and edgehold in technical terrain, and power when you’re snapping ollies. For some beginners, camber can be a touch trickier to learn on, and  can feel catchier than rocker. However, camber also helps snowboarders learn fundamentals the right way. You can’t cheat on a camber snowboard, whereas a rocker board is more forgiving and can subsequently let snowboarders develop sloppy habits. For that reason, we mostly recommend camber-dominant shapes in this guide, like our top pick, the Salomon Pulse and Lotus.

Rocker is the opposite of camber. The “U” is right side up this time, as rockered boards have one central point of contact with the ground between the feet, and the nose and tail are raised off the ground. Rocker-dominant boards, like the Rossignol Ampage Vol. 2, are less catchy than their camber counterparts, and they’re generally more playful, easier to butter, and more buoyant. However, they’re also less stable and don’t ollie as well.

Just like directional twins combine aspects from twins and directional shapes in search of a balanced ride, hybrid profiles merge attributes of camber and rocker. For instance, the CAPiTA Outerspace Living pairs grippy camber underfoot with catch-free, floaty rocker in the nose and tail.

Now that you’ve narrowed in on the board you want, you face an equally important question: What size should you get? Sizing a snowboard correctly can make or break your season, but the truth is that there’s no perfect formula. Here are a few things to consider to make the best possible choice for you.

Even as a beginner, you can learn about your preferences from snowboards you’ve already ridden. For instance, if you rented a 155 and it felt too squirrely and unstable, you might want to size up a touch. If you demoed a 160 and it felt too beefy and you weren’t able to turn it, sizing down is a smart idea. Especially as you’re progressing from a true beginner towards intermediate status, it’s important to demo lots of snowboards, as it’ll help you develop your personal taste and your snowboard vocabulary.

Always pay close attention to specs provided by manufacturers, as many brands provide helpful sizing parameters, including ranges for rider weight, boot size, and height. Use this information as a potential starting point.

Between two sizes? If you’re a beginner or riding park, gravitate toward the smaller size, which are easier to turn and spin. If you’re getting more advanced or you’re riding powder frequently, consider the bigger size, which provides more float and stability.

Some brands won’t give you a range of rider heights, but they will give you a range of rider weights, as weight influences how a rider engages flex and how much a board will float. For example, there are skinny 6’3” riders who like 158s, and squat, powerful 5’8” riders who dig 160s.

If you have average-sized feet for your height and weight, you don’t need to worry too much about boot compatibility. But if you have big feet—say, you’re 5’9” and 150 pounds, but you have a size 11.5 boot—it’s important to consider wider snowboards like the Cardiff Lynx. Wide boards help riders with big feet avoid dragging their toes in the snow, which is at best annoying and at worst can cause avoidable crashes. When you scan those manufacturer-provided specs, pay attention to the width and, if possible, ideal boot size ranges. It’s also worth noting that certain snowboards, like the Never Summer Proto Type 3, are available in standard sizes, as well as wide sizes, which Never Summer denotes with an “X.”

This past spring, we sent 44 snowboards out to testers across the Wasatch, Rockies, and Sierra. Those testers aren’t beginners—far from it. These are advanced to expert riders who live and breathe snowboarding, with a few progressing intermediates to mix things up. This crew is intimately familiar with snowboard design concepts, and many of them have been testing snowboards for a decade. A handful of testers are currently instructors or coaches, or they’ve held those jobs in the past. Needless to say, they know what beginners need under their boots to learn the ropes.

After testing snowboards at their home mountains all spring, those testers filled out detailed snowboard review forms. They scored boards quantitatively on aspects like flex, responsiveness, pop, playfulness, stability at speed, etcetera. Then they dove deep on additional qualitative questions, among them: Would you spend your hard-earned money on this board? What conditions or terrain does this board excel or struggle in?

And then, most importantly, we ask these testers to rewind the clock and think like a beginner. Will the board in question encourage a beginner to progress and step up their game? Is it easy to ride? Or is it a hard-to-command bronco that’ll buck a less experienced snowboarder?

Finally, we sifted through those reviews, compared our favorite snowboards of 2027 to old favorites from last year, and compiled the guide you just finished reading. Our hope is that this labor of love helps you enjoy your time on the slopes. Happy hunting—and happier shredding!

Drew Zieff is a Tahoe-based freelance writer and a lifelong snowboarder. He directs Outside’s snowboard test, a role he’s handled since 2016. He also directs Backcountry Magazine’s splitboard coverage and nerds out on snowboard gear and travel for Whitelines Snowboarding, The Snowboarder’s Journal, REI, Gear Junkie, among others. He spends his winters testing gear in his backyard backcountry zones or up at Palisades when he’s not chasing stories and storms around the globe.

In a word, Kordell Black is a character. An esoteric ripper and veteran gear tester based in the Wasatch, Black has traveled the world with his snowboard in pursuit of powder and off-the-beaten-track adventures. He approaches big mountain lines with creative, freestyle energy, specializes in finding powder stashes overlooked by the Utah crowds, and thrashes boots into oblivion in a matter of weeks. He will also obliterate you in a flat ground game of SKATE.

Truckee’s own Jenna Shlachter is as brilliant on the hill as she is off it—which is saying something since she’s a Doctor of Psychology, after all. When she’s not on the clock, she’s usually on her snowboard, ripping everything from park to powder. Her trophy shelf needs regular reinforcement, as every year she seems to add banked slalom awards to her already impressive collection. Lucky for us, Shlachter is also an unabashed snowboard nerd, and she’s been a key contributor to our snowboard test for the last few seasons.

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Christos Tzolis has revealed how Arsenal boss Mikel Arteta is different to any other manager he has played under.

The Greek joined the Gunners last summer in a £34million move from Club Brugge, that saw the winger return to England after a spell at Norwich City.

The forward had an underwhelming period at Carrow Road before moving to Belgium, where he lifted the Pro League title last season, which helped propel him to the Emirates Stadium.

He has just returned from a hamstring injury on international duty that kept him out for a month, but he made a return to action in the 1-0 win over Liverpool at Anfield on November 1.

Tzolis has spoken of his admiration for Arteta being a big part of why he wanted to move to north London, and he explained to Novasports how the Spaniard differed from any head coach he had had previously.

"He does a lot of things before the matches, some alert games, in the locker room to see the players' readiness,” he said.

“He always has different ideas before each game and approaches it differently.

"I mean, I haven't seen that with any other coach. In the first talk we had in the summer, he asked me about my life, about my life outside of the game as well, about football of course.

“And I spoke for 45 minutes, for sure, and 10-15 minutes as coach, so he was very open to hearing what I had to say."

SAS Boosts Flights To These 2 Major Delta Hubs For Summer 2027

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

SAS is increasing flights to two major Delta Air Lines hubs as part of an extensive expansion of its summer 2027 schedule. The Scandinavian carrier will increase services from Copenhagen Airport (CPH) to Atlanta and Seattle, strengthening connections with its US partner while adding capacity across a total of seven intercontinental routes.

The November 10 announcement also includes larger Airbus aircraft, more than 20 European and Scandinavian route enhancements, and two new destinations. The expansion follows SAS's transition from Star Alliance to SkyTeam, which has brought it closer to Delta and Air France-KLM.

Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) will increase from five weekly flights to a daily service from Copenhagen, representing 40% growth, while Seattle rises from four to five weekly flights. Both changes restore previously offered frequencies: SAS launched Atlanta flights in June 2024, shortly before its formal move to SkyTeam, wasting no time connecting into the alliance's busiest global hub. SAS also operates nonstop services to three other Delta hubs: Boston, Los Angeles and New York-JFK.

SAS' partnership with Delta formally began in September 2024, just days after joining the alliance, with a revised codeshare agreement. The expanded agreement covered 150 destinations in North America, as well as around 50 destinations in Northern Europe. The cooperation increased again in April 2025, with both carriers adding more connecting opportunities and reciprocal loyalty benefits. Paul Verhagen, Chief Commercial Officer at SAS, said at the time:

“We are thrilled to continue expanding our partnership with Delta, enhancing travel options between Scandinavia and North America. As a proud new member of SkyTeam Alliance, our collaboration offers great opportunities to provide seamless connectivity and benefits across our global network."

The relationship is expected to deepen further through Air France-KLM's proposed acquisition of a controlling 60.5% stake in SAS. During its November 5 earnings call, the group's CEO Benjamin Smith said the group now hopes to complete the transaction in the first quarter of 2027, subject to European Commission approval. This could eventually support SAS joining the existing Delta–Air France–KLM–Virgin Atlantic transatlantic joint venture.

Beyond Atlanta and Seattle, SAS will increase Copenhagen flights to Tokyo Haneda Airport (HND), Seoul's Incheon International Airport (ICN), and Washington Dulles International Airport (IAD) from six weekly to daily. Chicago and Mumbai will also receive more seats through aircraft upgauging, although SAS has not announced additional frequencies for these two routes.

The A350 upgauges add 34 seats per departure, with capacity rising from 266 to 300 passengers. Mumbai Airport (BOM) is particularly noteworthy, having only launched in October this year, marking SAS's return to India after 17 years. Previously operated five times weekly with the A330, the route will enter its first full summer season with larger aircraft, demonstrating the airline's longer-term ambitions in the subcontinent.

These changes precede SAS's largest-ever aircraft investment, announced in June of this year. The agreement covers up to 40 widebodies, including 18 firm Airbus A330-900 orders, two additional leased A330-900s, ten purchase rights, and up to ten additional Airbus A330-300s. The secondhand A330s will support near-term expansion, while new-generation aircraft will underpin fleet modernization. The investment has a stated list-price value exceeding $10 billion.

The carrier has put pen to paper on the largest investment in its history.

As if that wasn't enough, SAS is also strengthening its European and Scandinavian network to support Copenhagen's development as a major international connecting hub. Flights from Copenhagen to Stockholm Arlanda Airport (ARN) will increase from 15 to 18 daily departures, while Aarhus rises from four to five daily flights. Geneva, Barcelona and Venice will also gain significant additional capacity, improving connections between European destinations and SAS's expanding long-haul network.

Beyond these confirmed increases, SAS also plans more departures from Copenhagen to Nice, Palma de Mallorca, Porto, Bilbao and Catania, although frequencies are not yet confirmed. Stockholm will gain additional flights to Luleå, Kiruna, Milan and Madrid, while Geneva and Keflavik services will operate throughout the summer. Oslo will also see increased frequencies to Manchester, Brussels and Rome.

The expansion also introduces two new seasonal destinations from Copenhagen. Ljubljana Jože Pucnik Airport (LJU) launches June 25, operating twice-weekly until August 6 with a 122-seat Embraer E195. Menorca follows on June 30, with weekly flights until August 4 using a 180-seat Airbus A320neo. Menorca becomes SAS's third Balearic destination, while Ljubljana adds another European capital to its network. Together, the new additions reinforce Copenhagen's role as the airline's principal international hub.

3 Easy Hacks To Make Instant Mashed Potatoes WAY Better

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

You might prefer homemade mashed potatoes, but sometimes you just need a shortcut—and on days like that, grabbing the box is WAY easier. But while the back-of-the-box directions will yield just-fine results, a few easy hacks will give you much better results.

As a recipe developer and professional cook, I don’t often make instant mashed potatoes, but I was excited by the challenge of leveling them up—without adding a bunch of extra ingredients or steps that take the convenience out of this convenience food. So I tried making instant mashed potatoes every way I could think of (including the way the box says!). In the end, I found three tricks that, while super simple, massively improve that final bowl of mashed potatoes.

Here are three ways to instantly upgrade instant mashed potatoes.

Most boxes tell you to rehydrate instant mashed potato flakes in hot water enriched with a little butter and a few tablespoons of milk—but I’m going all in on the milk here. The extra dairy really fills out the flavor of the mashed potato and makes it creamier—especially if you’re using a higher-fat milk, like whole or 2%.

When swapping milk for water, it’s best to increase the liquid amount just slightly. For some reason (totally scientific, I’m sure!), the potato flakes require more milk than water to hydrate properly. I recommend 2 extra tablespoons for every half cup of instant mashed potatoes.

Also, add the extra milk the box typically calls for. If you leave it out, the potatoes will be dry. When I was testing, I found it didn’t really matter when that second amount of milk went into the pan (at the beginning versus just before the potatoes), but some boxes emphasize putting it in cold just before adding the potatoes—so if you want to do that to make you feel safer, be my guest!

All the instant mashed potatoes I made lacked seasoning—until I doubled the salt. Then they were perfect. Now here’s the thing: I was testing with Diamond Crystal kosher salt, which is less salty by volume than table salt or Morton’s kosher. The back of the instant mashed potato box didn’t specify what type of salt they used, so if they tested with a fine salt or Morton’s kosher, maybe ¼ teaspoon was the perfect amount. But in my kitchen with Diamond Crystal, it was woefully underseasoned.

Maybe you’re nervous about oversalting and don’t want to double or even slightly increase the salt right away. But regardless, I’m begging you: Taste those potatoes before you serve them and don’t feel bad if you have to add another hefty pinch.

You might assume that mashed potatoes should be made in a pot—I definitely did. But the fluffiest, creamiest instant potatoes I made in this whole testing process came from a wide, high-sided sauté pan. The reason for this? Absolute science.

When adding the instant potato flakes to the hot liquid, the goal is to distribute them as evenly as possible without working them too much. Yes, the back of the box says to “stir” the potatoes in, but in testing, I quickly discovered that even a few strokes can quickly lead to gummy potatoes.

Here’s where the pan size comes in. Using a wide pan increases the liquid's surface area, so you can sprinkle the potatoes evenly over the top and pat them down into the liquid instead of stirring. From there, let them stand until they’ve absorbed the liquid, then give them a few stirs. Trust me: They’ll be way fluffier because you messed with them less!

We Read Sofia Franklyn’s ‘Daddy Issues’ Memoir So You Don’t Have to—Here Are the 6 Biggest Bombshells

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The day has finally come. Sofia “With an F” Frankyln’s tell-all memoir, Daddy Issues, is on shelves now, which means a bunch of details from her infamous fallout with her former Call Her Daddy co-host and best friend, Alex Cooper, are coming to light. Seriously—there’s so much tea in these pages, it even prompted Alix Earle to pick up a copy and apparently read a book for the first time. (Her words, not mine!)

Aside from her reported feud with Alex, Sofia unveiled other shocking details from her personal life throughout the memoir. Here are the six biggest bombshells from Sofia Franklyn’s memoir, Daddy Issues.

Sofia truly wasted no time. In the prologue to her memoir, she revealed Alex’s husband, Matt Kaplan, allegedly called her book agent the day she announced Daddy Issues and threatened to take legal action. It was the first time she had (sort of) been contacted by Alex since their infamous fallout in 2020.

“I remember this feeling of powerlessness. Alex rearranging my life with no acknowledgment. My life being taken, overnight. On the scoreboard of Alex vs. Sofia, Alex won. I cannot fathom her trying to do it again,” Sofia wrote in her memoir. “I feel bulletproof… something born out of a dark place. For the first time I have something Alex wants. Our story. My story.”

Sofia admitted to her tumultuous journey with drugs and alcohol over the years, which eventually led to her multiple arrests. One of her mugshots was even printed on Call Her Daddy merch in June 2020.

She got arrested three separate times while she was in college. In one instance, her fake ID was denied at a bar. She was arrested again when she was caught underage drinking at a DJ set, and a third time when she got “so blackout [drunk]” she “can’t remember what happened.”

Before she married Matt in 2024, Alex was romantically linked to MLB player Noah Syndergaard. They had an on-again, off-again relationship in 2017, and Sofia claimed Alex had been plotting to get back at him when he attempted to reconcile with her over dinner.

“I have a favor to ask,” Alex said, per Sofia’s recollection. “Okay, we’re going to Lure Fishbar and I want you to get a picture.”

“Like, paparazzi-type shit?” Sofia asked, to which Alex allegedly responded, “He’s just such a shit, and I want to have them in case I need to send them to his mom.”

Sofia revealed the photo she took eventually ran in a January 2018 report from Busted Coverage, alleging Alex submitted the tip herself. “Who has access to this photo besides me and her??” she wrote.

According to the “anonymous” tip, Alex was described as “the smoking hot former Boston U soccer player who we’ve met and posted about multiple times.”

When CHD became one of the most popular pods in the biz, the former co-hosts pushed the company to renegotiate their 3-year contract early, asking for $1 million per year in a new contract.

At the time, Barstool maintained the intellectual property of CHD, which was also the main reason Alex and Sofia’s friendship and professional partnership ended. As the girls tried to figure out next steps, they nearly left the company and started their own show called The Fathers.

After Sofia drank heavily at the Barstool holiday party, she claimed she woke up the next morning with little to no recollection of the night before—but she knew she had done something “very wrong.”

When she asked Alex about it, the Unwell founder claimed her behavior was “rude” and “aggressive,” to the point that Dave Portnoy almost “fired” her. Sofia later learned that Alex attempted to use her alleged party fouls as leverage to get a promotion.

“She was like, ‘If I have to put up with her, I need more money,’” Alex’s friend and former CHD employee, Muji, reportedly told Sofia. “So she got the raise.”

In case you need a rundown, here’s what happened: Alex continued CHD under Barstool in 2021, after Sofia wanted to leave and start another podcast. Months after Sofia was left to her own devices, Barstool’s founder, Dave Portnoy, reportedly offered her a solo show.

“She came to me with a plan, and I needed an episode. There’s no clear favorite. You could always have your own show, and we promote the shit out of it like we did with Call Her Daddy. I think it would be huge,” Dave said, according to Sofia. She also claimed Alex had told Dave that she was “never going to sign” the Barstool contract because she was “trying to blow up the deal.”

Sofia never ended up taking his offer and started her own podcast, Sofia With an F, independently in October 2020.

As for Alex, she eventually left Barstool and took CHD with her, signing a $60 million deal with Spotify. She signed another $125 million deal with SiriusXM in 2024.

Dave and Alex have yet to respond to any of Sofia’s allegations in her memoir, if at all.

Resident Evil remakes skipped the first game because Capcom worried it "might not resonate"

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

"We felt that experiencing that was the best way to introduce the origins"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Capcom's modern Resident Evil remakes are fantastic entry points for one of the most beloved survival horror series of all time, but it's always felt weird that the very first 1996 game still hasn't been given the full-blown third-person RE Engine treatment. Yes, there's a remake from 2002, but it's way more faithful to the original game than the much more recent remakes of Resident Evil 2, 3, and 4, featuring the classic tank controls and static camera from the series' origin. Basically, if you want to start Resident Evil with the first game in the series, you have to deal with quite a few design foundations that'll feel seriously dated if you're unfamiliar. Like ink ribbons. You don't want ink ribbons, trust me.

With how successful the Resident Evil remakes have been, it may seem inevitable that Capcom revisits the very first game. But while rumors suggest there's already a new RE1 early in development, there's been no official announcement or even heavily substantiated reporting to add weight to those rumors. As to why Capcom didn't choose Resident Evil as the obvious starting point for its newer remakes, longtime series producer Jun Takeuchi says the company worried it wouldn't resonate with a modern audience.

"The reason we didn't choose Resident Evil 1 as the first remake is because it's such a stoic game," Takeuchi says in a new book celebrating the series' 30th anniversary (translation via Fandom). "Because it focuses so heavily on the horror derived from its stoicism, we were concerned that it might not resonate with today's game fans."

As is always the case with second-hand translations, it's hard to pin down the exact intent behind translated words like "stoic," but Resident Evil 1 definitely has a distinct vibe and slower pace than newer games in the series. Speaking of which, Takeuchi says the iconic opening sequence from Resident Evil 2, which sets up the mass infection of Raccoon City, is basically where everything kicks off anyway.

"Furthermore, the Raccoon City incident is essentially the starting point of the Resident Evil saga, so we felt that experiencing that was the best way to introduce the origins," he says.

I definitely don't disagree. Resident Evil 2 is arguably the most classic version of Resident Evil you can experience, whether that's the 1998 original or the 2019 remake. That said, as a lifelong fan and noted tank control hater, I would love to see a modern reimagining of the game that started it all.

Where does your favorite land on our ranking of the best Resident Evil games?
